Impulsive Release Strategies for Wolbachia-Infected Mosquitoes Under Temperature-Induced Infection Loss

Jéssica C. S. Alves, Christian E. Schaerer, Cláudia P. Ferreira

Abstract

The release of Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes is a promising strategy for controlling Aedes aegypti populations, although high-temperature exposure may temporarily reduce infection levels and compromise long-term persistence. We propose a population-dynamics model based on impulsive differential equations that incorporates cytoplasmic incompatibility, periodic mosquito releases, and temperature-driven infection loss. Analytical results establish fundamental qualitative properties of the continuous and impulsive models, derive conditions for the existence and stability of periodic solutions, and provide an explicit release threshold for the successful establishment and long-term persistence of the infected population. Numerical simulations validate the theoretical results and compare the performance of the wMelPop, wMel, and wAlbB strains under different thermal scenarios, highlighting the influence of strain-specific fitness costs and thermal tolerance. Additional simulations investigate temperature-dependent demographic variation and imperfect maternal transmission, extending the analysis beyond the assumptions of the analytical framework. The proposed approach provides a tractable mathematical framework for analyzing and designing impulsive Wolbachia release strategies under environmentally driven infection loss.

Introduction

Mosquito population control is a common strategy for reducing the transmission of vector-borne diseases. In this work, we specifically consider the dynamics of Aedes aegypti, the vector of several arboviruses, including dengue, Zika, chikungunya, and yellow fever (Ahebwa et al. ; Zardini et al. ). This species is now present in most tropical and subtropical regions and has the potential to establish itself in more than 80% of countries across the globe (Cheong et al. ; Lim et al. ). Current control methods include the use of insecticides, mechanical elimination of breeding sites, and biological control approaches (Dorigatti et al. ; Lees et al. ; Onen et al. ).

Biological control comprises several techniques, including the use of natural predators, parasitoids, and specific pathogens that affect mosquito development and survival. Among the most common approaches is the introduction of natural predators, such as certain fish species, including Gambusia affinis, which consume mosquito larvae in aquatic environments (Steven et al. ). Another strategy involves predatory mosquitoes and parasitoid insects: larvae of mosquitoes of the genus Toxorhynchites prey on other mosquito larvae (Sukupayo et al. ), while parasitoid wasps, such as Trichogramma, oviposit on mosquito eggs, contributing to population suppression (Parra and Coelho ). Additionally, insecticides remain an important component of mosquito control. However, large-scale application raises environmental concerns, including the evolution of insecticide resistance, which has been reported in several countries and compromises the effectiveness of mosquito-borne disease control efforts (Carson et al. ).

In addition to these biological and chemical approaches, genetic control techniques, such as the Sterile Insect Technique (SIT), have been widely applied in the management of agricultural pests (Zheng et al. ) and adapted to mosquito sterilization (Almeida et al. ; Bliman et al. ; Huang et al. , ; Li and Ai ). Finally, an emerging biological control strategy involves the release of mosquitoes carrying Wolbachia, a symbiotic bacterium that reduces mosquito vector competence and limits arbovirus transmission without requiring genetic modification of the mosquito population (Dorigatti et al. ; Ross ; Pinto et al. ; Dos Santos et al. ; Zara et al. ; Gesto et al. ).

The interaction between wild mosquitoes and those infected with Wolbachia or genetically modified mosquitoes has been extensively investigated in the literature (Almeida et al. ; Bliman et al. ; Gonzales et al. ; Orozco-Gonzales et al. ; Almeida et al. ; Cardona-Salgado et al. ; Lopes et al. ; Yu and Zheng ). Most of this literature relies on mathematical modeling to analyze population dynamics, employing systems of ordinary differential equations (Almeida et al. , ; Campo-Duarte et al. ; Benedito et al. ), delay differential equations (Lopes et al. ; Santos Benedito et al. ; Li and Ai ), and discrete-time formulations (Blackwood et al. ; Ufuktepe ; Yu and Zheng ), among others. In addition, optimal control frameworks have been widely adopted to design efficient intervention strategies (Almeida et al. ; Gonzales et al. ; Campo-Duarte et al. , ; Almeida et al. ). These modeling approaches aim to reduce the wild mosquito population and, consequently, mitigate disease transmission.

While much of the existing work focuses on continuous release strategies of Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes (Cardona-Salgado et al. ; Lopes et al. ; Benedito et al. ; Ogunlade et al. ), alternative formulations based on impulsive releases have also been proposed (Gonzales et al. ; Almeida et al. ; Li et al. ; Liu et al. ; Dianavinnarasi et al. ). Periodic and impulsive release strategies appear more frequently in the context of sterile insect techniques (Huang et al. ; Li and Ai ) and in applications to other insect populations and pest control problems (Liu et al. ; Alves et al. ; Pei et al. ; Wang et al. ). These approaches are designed to optimize the timing and magnitude of releases, enhancing control efficiency and improving the feasibility of real-world interventions. Field deployment programs have also involved additional or supplementary releases to support the establishment and persistence of Wolbachia-infected mosquito populations (Ryan et al. ; Nazni et al. ; Velez et al. ).

This work specifically addresses the control of Aedes aegypti, with a particular focus on utilizing the endosymbiotic bacterium Wolbachia as a biological control strategy. Several experimental and field studies have shown that the release of Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes can substantially reduce vector competence and, consequently, the transmission of arboviruses (Ross ; Dos Santos et al. ; Zara et al. ). These findings support the use of Wolbachia as an effective tool for the long-term control of Aedes aegypti populations (Pavan et al. ).

In this article, we model the interaction between wild Aedes aegypti mosquitoes and mosquitoes carrying the Wolbachia bacterium, incorporating the mechanism of cytoplasmic incompatibility, which reduces the reproductive success of uninfected females when mating with infected males (Kaur et al. ). The model also includes two impulsive events: one representing the loss of Wolbachia infection due to high-temperature exposure, and the other corresponding to the periodic release of Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes. Within this framework of impulsive differential equations, we analyze the system dynamics to determine conditions that ensure the fixation of Wolbachia-carrying mosquitoes in the population.

Field and laboratory evidence indicate that high temperatures can temporarily reduce Wolbachia density and weaken cytoplasmic incompatibility in Aedes aegypti, particularly in environments subject to intense thermal fluctuations (Ross et al. ; Gunasekaran et al. ). For the wMel strain, heatwaves have been associated with decreases in infection frequencies, with values falling to 83% in larvae sampled directly from field habitats and to 88% in eggs collected from ovitraps, before recovering toward baseline levels in subsequent months (Ross et al. ). Experimental studies also report drastic reductions in Wolbachia density when immature stages experience elevated temperatures, with levels dropping to below 0.1% of controls after seven days of heat exposure beginning at the egg stage, followed by partial recovery once temperatures return to milder ranges (Gunasekaran et al. ; Ulrich et al. ). These observations motivate the inclusion in our model of a redistributive impulsive event representing episodic loss of infection due to high-temperature exposure, capturing reductions in the proportion of infected mosquitoes, transferring this proportion to the wild mosquito population, and allowing for their gradual reestablishment over time.

Temperature effects, however, are strain-dependent, with direct implications for the magnitude and frequency of the impulsive loss term in the proposed model. The wMel strain, while capable of blocking dengue and Zika transmission, shows sensitivity to elevated temperatures and exhibits reduced stability under heat stress (Ross et al. ; Ulrich et al. ; Ant et al. ). The wMelPop strain is even more susceptible, displaying pronounced fitness costs and sharp declines in density when exposed to high temperatures (Ross et al. , ), suggesting a larger and more frequent infection-loss impulse. In contrast, the wAlbB strain demonstrates greater tolerance to thermal stress, maintaining comparatively higher densities under fluctuating or elevated temperatures (Gunasekaran et al. ; Mancini et al. ), implying a weaker impulsive reduction in infection levels. Since both pathogen-blocking capacity and thermal resilience influence persistence and replacement dynamics, we summarize these characteristics in Table to support the interpretation of the model parameters and their biological relevance.Table 1Key biological traits of Wolbachia strains: virus blocking, heat tolerance, and fitness effectsStrainVirus blockHeat toleranceImpact on host fitnessReferenceswMelPopVery highLowStrong reduction in fecundity, egg viability, and longevity(Ross et al. , ; Ritchie et al. )wMelModerateLowModerate reduction in fecundity and egg viability; little effect on longevity(Ulrich et al. ; Mancini et al. ; Maciel-de-Freitas et al. )wAlbBHighModerate/HighMild effects; fertility may drop under heat stress(Gunasekaran et al. ; Ant et al. ; Axford et al. )

To address this problem, we formulate a population dynamics model governed by impulsive differential equations that incorporates two distinct impulsive mechanisms: the periodic release of Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes and the temperature-driven loss of infection. This modeling approach extends existing impulsive frameworks by coupling operational interventions with environmentally induced perturbations. We establish analytical conditions for the existence, characterization, and stability of periodic solutions, and derive explicit threshold conditions that link the magnitude and timing of release events to the intensity and frequency of thermal infection loss. These results provide theoretical criteria guaranteeing long-term persistence of Wolbachia infection in the mosquito population.

In addition to the theoretical analysis, we perform numerical simulations to validate the derived threshold conditions and to investigate how episodic high-temperature events influence infection dynamics. The simulations illustrate the interplay between release schedules, environmental stress, and strain-specific biological traits, while also examining two biologically relevant extensions that are not included in the analytical framework, namely temperature-dependent demographic variation and imperfect maternal transmission. These additional experiments provide further insight into the sensitivity of the proposed release strategy to assumptions commonly adopted in mathematical models of Wolbachia population replacement.

The main contributions of this work are the analytical characterization of the impulsive model and the derivation of an explicit threshold for the number of Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes that must be released at each intervention time τ τ. This threshold guarantees the successful establishment of the infected mosquito population and the elimination of the wild population in the absence of temperature-induced infection loss. Furthermore, when episodic infection loss due to high temperatures is incorporated into the model, the obtained condition ensures that the wild mosquito population can be maintained arbitrarily close to zero, provided that the release magnitude satisfies the derived threshold. Complementing the analytical results, additional numerical simulations investigate temperature-dependent demographic variation and imperfect maternal transmission, demonstrating how these biologically relevant extensions influence infection persistence and the effectiveness of the proposed release strategy.

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. Section presents the interaction model between wild and Wolbachia-infected Aedes aegypti mosquitoes, establishing the fundamental properties of the solutions, including existence, uniqueness, positivity, boundedness, and the local stability of equilibrium states. Section introduces the impulsive population-dynamic model and provides the theoretical analysis of the coupled impulsive system, where both release interventions and temperature-induced infection loss are formally described. In this section, we derive threshold conditions ensuring long-term infection persistence. Section presents numerical simulations supporting the analytical results, compares the performance of different Wolbachia strains under distinct temperature-induced infection-loss scenarios, and investigates the effects of temperature-dependent demographic variation and imperfect maternal transmission. Finally, Sect. summarizes the main conclusions and discusses the applicability and limitations of the proposed framework.

The model in () was constructed to capture the main biological mechanisms governing the interaction between wild and Wolbachia-infected Aedes aegypti populations. The logistic-type terms reflect the limitation imposed by resource competition, assuming that both populations share the same environmental carrying capacity K. This shared constraint expresses the ecological competition for breeding sites and food resources, which are finite within the habitat.

The multiplicative structure of the growth terms ensures that population change is proportional to the current population size, following standard formulations in population dynamics. The additional factor\documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}

 \usepackage{amsbsy}

 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$S_1$$\end{document}S1 represents the cytoplasmic incompatibility (CI) effect: when Wolbachia-infected males mate with wild females, a fraction γ γ of the eggs fail to develop (Kaur et al. ). Thus, (1-γ )(1-γ) quantifies the viable offspring fraction produced in mixed matings, reducing the reproductive success of the wild population.

The infected population, on the other hand, does not experience this reduction, as Wolbachia transmission is assumed to be maternal and, in this case, fully efficient. Therefore, the growth of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

 \begin{document}$$\psi _2$$\end{document}ψ2 and limited by the same carrying capacity K. Maternal transmission of Wolbachia is assumed to be perfect, and therefore, vertical transmission failure is not considered. Possible mechanisms leading to infection loss are introduced and discussed in the following section.

The assumption of perfect maternal transmission is adopted here as an idealized baseline, commonly used in models of Wolbachia dynamics under stable environmental conditions. However, experimental evidence indicates that elevated temperatures may reduce Wolbachia density and compromise vertical transmission, leading to the production of uninfected offspring by infected females.

In the present model, these effects are not explicitly included in the continuous dynamics, being represented in an aggregate way through the impulsive infection-loss term. A limitation of this modeling approach is that it does not capture the generational effects associated with imperfect maternal inheritance, which may lead to optimistic predictions of persistence under strong thermal stress. To evaluate the impact of this assumption, simulations incorporating a transmission efficiency parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\xi \in (0,1]$$\end{document}ξ∈(0,1] are presented in Sect. .

The model is not stage-structured and therefore does not explicitly describe the effects of thermal stress on eggs, larvae, or pupae. Experimental evidence (Ross et al. ; Gunasekaran et al. ) indicates that these stages may be particularly sensitive to high temperatures, which can affect Wolbachia density and the infection frequency observed in the adult population.

In this context, the impulsive redistribution term should be interpreted as an aggregate representation of temperature-induced infection loss over the development process. As a result, the model cannot distinguish how thermal stress acting on different developmental stages contributes to the observed reduction in infection frequency. Addressing this question would require a stage-structured model, which is beyond the scope of the present work.

For this model, we consider the following assumptions:2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

Additionally, we assume3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}

This assumption is biologically realistic, as infection typically imposes a fitness cost on mosquitoes, reducing reproductive rates and increasing mortality. However, depending on the Wolbachia strain, these effects may vary significantly: some strains cause substantial reductions in mosquito fitness, while others exhibit milder impacts or even confer slight advantages under specific environmental conditions.

The interaction between ecological competition and cytoplasmic incompatibility determines whether the infection can coexist with or replace the wild population. The infection’s potential to replace the wild population depends on the interplay between the fitness cost and the reproductive advantage conferred by CI. Under suitable conditions, the system in () can exhibit coexistence or complete replacement of the wild population by the infected one, which will be explored in the subsequent analysis.

Having established the existence, uniqueness, positivity, and boundedness of solutions, we now investigate the equilibrium points and their stability properties. This analysis characterizes the long-term behavior of the interacting populations and identifies the conditions leading to coexistence or complete population replacement.

The conditions derived above admit a clear biological interpretation. The threshold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

The results established in this section characterize the qualitative behavior of the continuous model, including the existence, boundedness, and stability of its equilibrium. Together with the phase portraits, they provide the analytical foundation for the impulsive model introduced in the next section.

Impulsive Model

Given our objective of studying and formulating release strategies for Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes in a specific region, we adopt the framework of impulsive differential equations due to its practical relevance in modeling discontinuous interventions. In this model, we consider two types of periodic impulses: (i) the seasonal loss of infection caused by temperature variations and (ii) the impulsive release of infected mosquitoes.

We consider a fixed release interval τ >0τ>0, corresponding to the time between successive mosquito release events. To represent the effects of seasonal temperature variation, we assume that Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes may lose the infection when exposed to elevated temperatures. This process is modeled through a redistributive impulse occurring at times t=(n+l)τ t=(n+l)τ, where l∈ [0,1]l∈[0,1] specifies the relative timing of the seasonal infection-loss event within each release cycle.  \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {Z}_+$$\end{document}n∈Z+ denotes the release cycle. When l=0l=0 or l=1l=1, the infection-loss event coincides with a mosquito release. At these instants, a proportion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

The impulsive representation is adopted as a mathematical approximation of the temperature-induced infection loss process. Although temperature varies continuously throughout the year, the model aggregates its effect into infection-loss events occurring at specified times within the release cycles. Thus, the impulsive event does not imply that Wolbachia loss occurs instantaneously in natural mosquito populations; rather, it represents the cumulative effect of temperature-induced infection loss over the corresponding period. Since the present study does not use real temperature data, the function φ (t)φ(t) is not intended to reproduce the exact temperature-dependent infection-loss dynamics observed in natural populations. Instead, it provides a tractable framework for investigating how seasonal infection loss may interact with periodic releases of infected mosquitoes (Fig. ).Fig. 2Seasonal function φ (t)φ(t) defined in () plotted for three different values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

Within the impulsive differential equations framework adopted in this study, τ τ denotes the fixed time interval between successive releases of Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes. Seasonal infection-loss events occur at shifted impulsive times of the form (n+l)τ (n+l)τ and, depending on the value of l, may coincide with the release instants. In real-world applications, the release size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$u_n$$\end{document}un is naturally constrained by the limited availability of Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes. To reflect this practical limitation, we define the set of admissible release sizes as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$U:= \{ 0 \le u_n\le u_{\text {max}}, \mid n \ge 0 \}$$\end{document}U:={0≤un≤umax,∣n≥0}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$u_{\text {max}} \ge 0$$\end{document}umax≥0 denotes the maximum number of Wolbachia-carrying mosquitoes that can be introduced at any given intervention.

It is worth noting that temperature effects are incorporated in the present model exclusively through the infection-loss term φ (t)φ(t), while the demographic parameters are assumed constant. This simplification allows the analytical treatment developed in the following sections, although temperature may also affect mosquito life-history traits such as egg viability, development, and adult survival. To evaluate the impact of this assumption, additional numerical simulations incorporating temperature-dependent demographic parameters are presented in Sect. .

Despite the simplifying assumptions adopted in the model, the proposed impulsive framework captures two key features of Wolbachia-based population replacement programs: periodic releases of infected mosquitoes and temperature-induced infection loss. This formulation provides a tractable framework for investigating how release strategies interact with environmental stress while preserving the essential biological mechanisms governing Wolbachia establishment and persistence.

Establishing the uniform boundedness of the solutions is a crucial step in our analysis. Since we are dealing with population dynamics, it is natural to require that the trajectories remain biologically meaningful, that is, confined to a finite range for all time. Moreover, this property plays a central role in proving the global stability of the desired solutions, as it prevents the system from exhibiting unrealistic or uncontrolled behavior. Thus, after constructing and analyzing the auxiliary system, we now proceed to the proposition that ensures the uniform boundedness of the solutions of the impulsive model.

Finally, since the vector field of the continuous dynamics is locally Lipschitz and the jump maps either preserve S or map bounded sets into bounded sets, no finite-time blow-up can occur. Therefore, the solutions of the complete system is uniformly bounded. □ □

Having described the qualitative behavior of the model and the mechanisms governing the interaction between the two species, we now move to the formal analysis of the relevant equilibria in the impulsive regime. In particular, we examine the existence and stability of periodic solutions associated with the Wild-free state, in which the wild population of infected mosquitoes is eliminated, as well as the behavior of solutions that remain close to this state when small redistributive perturbations are introduced, namely, the impulses modeling the loss of infection due to high temperatures. In the next section, we establish conditions ensuring the existence of a positive, periodic, Wild-free solution and show that such a solution remains stable even in the presence of sufficiently small redistributive impulses. These results provide the necessary foundation for understanding how the system responds to perturbations and support the analysis of global stability around the dynamics we aim to achieve.

Global Stability of Solutions

In this section, we investigate the existence and stability of the periodic solutions that constitute the main focus of this work, particularly the wild-mosquito-free solution, which represents the desired state in which the wild mosquito population is eliminated while the population of Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes remains periodically stable. We first establish the existence of this periodic solution for the unperturbed system and derive a sufficient condition ensuring its local stability through Floquet theory. We then show that this solution persists under sufficiently small redistributive impulses by means of the Implicit Function Theorem. Finally, we prove that the wild-mosquito-free periodic solution is globally asymptotically stable in the absence of redistributive impulses and that the perturbed periodic solution remains asymptotically stable for sufficiently small values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\varphi _{\max }$$\end{document}φmax.

From a biological perspective, establishing the existence and stability of these solutions is essential for assessing the feasibility of population management strategies. The wild-mosquito-free solution corresponds to the scenario in which the wild mosquito population is suppressed while the population of Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes persists over time, one of the central goals of this study. Demonstrating that this state not only exists but also remains stable under small seasonal perturbations indicates that moderate environmental fluctuations, seasonal variations, and limited redistributive events do not compromise the long-term suppression of the wild mosquito population. Thus, these theoretical results provide mathematical support for the robustness of the proposed control strategy under biologically realistic intervention scenarios.

Numerical Simulations

The numerical simulations presented in this section investigate the behavior of the three Wolbachia strains (wMelPop, wMel, and wAlbB) under temperature-dependent infection loss. Particular emphasis is placed on evaluating the proposed release strategy, comparing the long-term performance of the different strains, and examining whether the main analytical conclusions remain valid under biologically relevant extensions of the model.

Unless otherwise stated, releases are performed during the first 365 days and the system is subsequently simulated for an additional 730 days, resulting in a total simulation period of three years. The complete parameterization and simulation scenarios are summarized in Tables , , , , and . Dynamics After the Releases Phase

In Figs. , , and , we examine the system’s behavior over three years, assuming that Wolbachia-infected females are released only during the first year. After this intervention phase, the system evolves without further releases, and the only remaining impulses are those associated with temperature-induced infection loss. We consider τ =14τ=14 and investigate three scenarios corresponding to low, moderate, and high temperature-induced infection loss.

Scenario 1: Low Infection Loss

Across the three scenarios, the long-term persistence of Wolbachia after the end of the releases is strongly shaped by the magnitude of temperature-induced infection loss. Under low and moderate thermal stress (Scenarios 1 and 2), all three strains maintain a stable infected population and keep the wild population suppressed throughout the three-year period. However, when the loss rate becomes high (Scenario 3), the differences between strains become pronounced: wMelPop and wMel are unable to withstand the strong thermal pressure and rapidly lose their infected populations once releases stop, whereas wAlbB remains resilient and maintains infection persistence. Our numerical findings are qualitatively consistent with experimental and field observations showing that elevated temperatures reduce Wolbachia density and weaken cytoplasmic incompatibility, particularly for heat-sensitive strains such as wMel (Ross et al. ; Gunasekaran et al. ; Ross et al. ). This biological behavior motivated the temperature-driven impulsive mechanism incorporated into our model. Consistent with these observations, our simulations predict long-term persistence for all strains under low and moderate thermal stress, whereas under high thermal stress only wAlbB remains established, reflecting its greater thermo tolerance.

Robustness to Temperature and Imperfect Maternal Transmission

The analytical framework developed in Sects. and incorporates temperature effects exclusively through the infection-loss function φ (t)φ(t) and assumes perfect maternal transmission of Wolbachia. Although these assumptions allow the analytical treatment of the model, experimental and field studies suggest that elevated temperatures may also affect mosquito demographic traits, while imperfect maternal transmission may occur under adverse environmental conditions.

To assess the robustness of our conclusions with respect to these assumptions, we consider two extensions of the model. First, the demographic parameters (birth and mortality rates) are allowed to vary seasonally with temperature. Second, maternal transmission is assumed to be imperfect, so that only a fraction of the offspring produced by infected females inherit the infection. These extensions considerably increase the complexity of the model and fall outside the analytical framework developed in Sect. . Therefore, no analytical results are derived for these modified systems, and their effects are investigated exclusively through numerical simulations.

To incorporate temperature effects into the demographic parameters, we introduce the normalized periodic function\documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$ f(t)=\frac{\left( 1+\cos \left( \frac{2\pi (t-t_{\textrm{peak}})}{T_c}\right) \right) ^2}{4}, $$\end{document}f(t)=1+cos2π(t-tpeak)Tc24,which is obtained from the same seasonal signal used in the definition of the infection-loss function φ (t)φ(t). Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$0\le f(t)\le 1$$\end{document}0≤f(t)≤1, the demographic parameters are modified according to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$ \psi _i(t)=\psi _i\bigl (1-\varepsilon f(t)\bigr ), \qquad \delta _i(t)=\delta _i\bigl (1+\varepsilon f(t)\bigr ), $$\end{document}ψi(t)=ψi(1-εf(t)),δi(t)=δi(1+εf(t)),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \in [0,1)$$\end{document}ε∈[0,1) measures the intensity of the temperature-dependent demographic variation. Thus, periods of stronger thermal stress simultaneously reduce birth rates and increase mortality rates. The impulsive mechanism associated with infection loss remains unchanged, so that seasonality affects the dynamics through both demographic variation and periodic infection loss.

To account for imperfect maternal transmission, we introduce a transmission efficiency parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}

 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\xi \in (0,1]$$\end{document}ξ∈(0,1]. Under this assumption, only a fraction ξ ξ of the offspring produced by infected females inherit Wolbachia, whereas the remaining fraction 1-ξ 1-ξ is recruited into the uninfected population. Accordingly, the recruitment term associated with infected mosquitoes,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$ \psi _2(t)S_2\left( 1-\frac{S_1+S_2}{K}\right) , $$\end{document}ψ2(t)S21-S1+S2K,is partitioned as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}

 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}

 \usepackage{upgreek}

To assess the influence of temperature-dependent demographic variation, simulations are performed for different values of the sensitivity parameter ɛ ε, while maintaining perfect maternal transmission (ξ =1ξ=1). The results are presented in Fig. .Fig. 10Population dynamics under temperature-dependent demographic variation for different values of ɛ ε with perfect maternal transmission (ξ =1ξ=1). The initial condition is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$(S_1(0),S_2(0))=(400,0)$$\end{document}(S1(0),S2(0))=(400,0). Periodic releases are performed every 14 days until day 365 using strain-specific release sizes of u=691u=691 (wMelPop), u=117u=117 (wMel), and u=94u=94 (wAlbB) (Color figure online)[11538_2026_1761_Fig10_HTML.webp]

Figure shows that incorporating temperature-dependent demographic variation modifies the population dynamics, with more pronounced effects as ɛ ε increases. For wMel and wAlbB, although seasonal fluctuations become more evident during periods of higher thermal stress, the qualitative dynamics of the original model are preserved, and the infected population remains established after releases cease. In contrast, wMelPop exhibits a different outcome for ɛ =0.6ε=0.6: the infected population is unable to maintain establishment and eventually declines to zero, indicating a reversal of the replacement process. This behavior suggests that the higher demographic costs associated with wMelPop, when further amplified by temperature-dependent reductions in birth rates and increases in mortality rates, may compromise the persistence of the infected population under stronger demographic fluctuations.

While the previous simulations isolate the effects of temperature-dependent demographic variation under perfect maternal transmission, we next investigate the combined effects of temperature-dependent demographic changes and imperfect maternal transmission. This scenario provides a more comprehensive assessment of the persistence of Wolbachia-infected populations when both demographic performance and infection inheritance are affected.

To examine these combined effects, the demographic sensitivity parameter was fixed at ɛ =0.5ε=0.5, while the maternal transmission efficiency ξ ξ was varied. In addition to perfect transmission ξ =1.0ξ=1.0, the values ξ =0.9ξ=0.9 and ξ =0.8ξ=0.8 were considered to explore the progressive loss of persistence as transmission efficiency decreases. In particular, ξ =0.8ξ=0.8 was included to identify a regime in which persistence fails even for the strains that remain established at higher transmission efficiencies. The corresponding results are shown in Fig. .Fig. 11Population dynamics for different values of the maternal transmission efficiency ξ ξ with ɛ =0.5ε=0.5. The initial condition is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$(S_1(0),S_2(0))=(400,0)$$\end{document}(S1(0),S2(0))=(400,0). Periodic releases are performed every 14 days until day 365 using strain-specific release sizes of u=691u=691 (wMelPop), u=117u=117 (wMel), and u=94u=94 (wAlbB) (Color figure online)[11538_2026_1761_Fig11_HTML.webp]

For ξ =1.0ξ=1.0, all three strains remain established after the release program despite the seasonal variation in demographic parameters. When the transmission efficiency is reduced to ξ =0.9ξ=0.9, wMel and wAlbB remain established, whereas wMelPop is unable to maintain the infection, reflecting its higher demographic fitness costs. A more critical regime emerges for ξ =0.8ξ=0.8, for which none of the three strains maintains long-term persistence: after releases cease, the infected populations progressively decline and the wild populations recover, indicating a reversal of the replacement process. Thus, the simultaneous action of temperature-dependent demographic changes and reduced maternal transmission can compromise population replacement even for wMel and wAlbB, which remain established under less severe conditions. These results emphasize that the combined effects of demographic and transmission-related mechanisms may produce qualitatively different establishment outcomes from those observed when each mechanism is considered under more favorable conditions.

Taken together, these numerical experiments show that temperature-dependent demographic variation can alter both seasonal population dynamics and, under stronger perturbations, the persistence of strains with higher fitness costs. More importantly, the combined action of demographic variation and imperfect maternal transmission can lead to a broader loss of infection persistence and reversal of the replacement process. These findings highlight the importance of considering the simultaneous effects of temperature on demographic performance and infection inheritance when assessing the long-term establishment of Wolbachia-infected populations.

Final Remarks

This work combined analytical results and numerical simulations to investigate impulsive release strategies of Aedes aegypti mosquitoes infected with Wolbachia under temperature-dependent infection loss. The results show that long-term infection persistence emerges from the interaction between strain-specific biological characteristics, thermal stress, and operational release parameters.

The analytical results provide conditions for the existence and stability of periodic solutions and establish a release threshold associated with successful population replacement. The numerical simulations support these results and show that strain-specific fitness costs strongly influence the required release effort. In particular, wMelPop requires substantially larger releases than wMel and wAlbB, whereas wAlbB exhibits greater resilience under increasing thermal stress. Increasing the release interval and the intensity of temperature-induced infection loss further challenges long-term establishment, particularly after the release program ceases.

The model was deliberately formulated within a relatively simple framework that preserves analytical tractability while capturing the population-level mechanisms associated with impulsive releases and temperature-induced infection loss. This structure makes it possible to derive analytical conditions and thresholds that would be considerably more difficult to obtain in a more detailed formulation. However, some biologically relevant mechanisms, including temperature-dependent demographic variation and imperfect maternal transmission, are not explicitly incorporated into the analytical model. Their effects were therefore explored numerically.

These additional simulations reveal that relaxing the baseline assumptions can affect both population fluctuations and long-term establishment. Under perfect maternal transmission, temperature-dependent demographic variation primarily modifies the seasonal dynamics of wMel and wAlbB, while stronger demographic perturbations can compromise the persistence of wMelPop, which is associated with higher fitness costs. When demographic variation is combined with imperfect maternal transmission, more substantial changes in persistence emerge. In particular, reduced transmission efficiency can lead to the loss of infection even for wMel and wAlbB and to a reversal of the replacement process. Thus, the combined action of demographic and transmission-related effects may produce establishment outcomes that are not observed under the assumptions of the analytical framework.

These findings illustrate the complementary roles of analytical and numerical approaches in the present study. The analytical model provides a tractable framework for identifying mechanisms and deriving conditions governing Wolbachia establishment, whereas the extended numerical simulations allow the consequences of additional biological mechanisms to be examined without substantially increasing the complexity of the analytical formulation. Together, these approaches help identify both the conditions favoring successful population replacement and scenarios in which persistence may be compromised.

As with any mathematical model, the applicability of the results depends on the biological context and the quality of the available parameter estimates. The proposed framework should therefore be viewed as a tool for investigating release strategies and exploring potential intervention scenarios rather than as a source of precise predictions for every field setting. Future applications should be calibrated using local entomological and environmental data to ensure that the model adequately represents the population dynamics of the target region.

Future work may include extending the analytical framework to explicitly incorporate temperature-dependent demographic parameters and imperfect maternal transmission, as well as the incorporation of spatial heterogeneity, stochastic temperature variation, stage-structured mosquito populations, and optimal control approaches to further improve the design and evaluation of Wolbachia-based release strategies under realistic environmental conditions.
